eṉil, atuvumillai. oru keṭṭavaṉ ettaṉaimuṟai vēṇṭumāṉālun tāṉ cākumaṭṭum makaḷir palarai maṇantukoḷḷalām; vēcivīṭṭilēyē kuṭiyāyirukkalām; taṉakkuḷḷa poruḷai yellāṅ kuṭikkuṅ kūttukkuñ cūtukkum vampuvaḻakkukkum celavaḻittuviṭṭut, tam maṉaivi makkaḷaic cōṟṟukkun tuṇikkum alaintu tiriyaviṭalām; tāṉ eḻupatu āṇṭu āṉālum patiṉaintāṇṭuḷḷa oru ciṟumiyai maṇantu koḷḷalām. ivaikaḷaik kēṭpārillai. āṉāl, ivaṉukkup piṇaikkappaṭṭa ēḻaicciṟumiyō ikkoṭiya cātikkiḻavaṉaiviṭṭu vilakuvaḷāyiṉ, avaḷ uṭaṉē kaṟpoḻukkattil vaḻuviyavaḷ āyviṭuvaḷām! aiyakō! avaṉukkut tāliyaṟutta avaḷ piṟaku vēṟoruvaṉai maṇantu koḷḷalākātām! atu cātikkuṟaivām! peṇmakkaḷai ī eṟumpu kocukupōl nacukkum ittaṉai cātik koṭumaikaḷ ōrālilai mūkkuppōlirukkum icciṟiya teṉṉāṭṭi laṉṟi, ālilaipōṟ paranta vēṟenta nāṭṭiluṅ kāṇap paṭuvatillai. maṟṟai nāṭukaḷil uḷḷa peṇpālār āṇpālārōṭoppa ellā urimaikaḷum peṟṟu iṉitu vāḻntuvarutalāl, avarkaḷ vayiṟṟiṟ piṟakkum piḷḷaikaḷum aṟivilum aḻakilum āṇmaiyilum piṟa nalaṅkiḷiluñ ciṟantu ulakiṟ payaṉ paṭṭu vāḻkiṉṟaṉar. tuṉpattil uḻalum nam intumātar kaḷiṉ vayiṟṟiṟ piṟakkum piḷkaiḷō aṟivillā maṭaiyarāy aḻakillā mucukkaḷāy avaṟṟiṟkēṟṟa tīya taṉmaiyun tīya ceyalumuṭaiyarāyt tuṉpavāḻviṟpaṭṭu viraivil uyirmāḷukiṉṟaṉar! ivvaḷāvuñ cāti vēṟṟumaiyāl viḷainta tuviṉaiyākum! oru peṇṇukku ēṟṟa iḷaiñaṉ enta marapiṟ piṟantavaṉā yiruntālum, avaḷ avaṉai virumpuvāḷāyiṉ avaṉukkē avaḷai vāḻkkaippaṭuttutal vēṇṭum. appōtutāṉ makaḷirkkuk kaṟpoḻukkam nilaipeṟum. ēlātavaṉ oruvaṉukkup piṇaikkappaṭṭavaḷ taṉakkēṟṟavaṉaik kāṇa neruṅkāl avaṉai virumpātu irāḷ. iḵtu ellā makkaḷiṭattum māṟṟamuṭiyāta iyaṟkaiyāka iṟaivaṉāl amaikkappaṭṭirukkiṉṟatu. muṟṟuntuṟanta muṉivarkaḷum, makkaḷiṉuñ ciṟanta tēvarkaḷum ivviyaṟkaiyaik kaṭakkamāṭṭātu. tattaḷittaṉareṉṟāl, eḷiya makkaṭ piṟappiṉarāṉa ēḻai iḷampeṇkaḷaik kuṟṟañ collutal aṭukkumō! ōr iḷaiñaṉ taṉakku icaiyātavaḷait, taṉakkumēl āṇṭiṉ mūtta oru kiḻaviyai maṇakkumpaṭi kaṭṭāyappaṭuttappaṭṭāl, avaṉ uḷḷam eppaṭik kotikkum! avaṉ avvēṟpāṭṭukku iṇaṅki naṭappaṉā? appaṭittāṉē ēḻai yiḷam peṇkaḷ maṉamum irukkum! ātalāṟ peṇmakkaḷai avar tamakku ēṟaṟa āṇmakkaḷōṭu maṇañ ceyvittalē uṭaṉē ceyaṟpālatu. cātik kaṭṭuppāṭukaḷāl makaḷir oḻukkap piḻaipaṭutalum, ataṉāṟ pala tīṅkukaḷ viḷaitalum nāḷukkunāṭ perukukiṉṟaṉa! oru ciṟu cātikkuḷḷēyē nalla maṇavāḷaṉ kiṭaitta laritu; oruvaṉ iruntāṟ peṇkaḷaippeṟṟōriṟ celvarāy uḷḷōr pōṭṭipōṭṭukkoṇṭu āyirakkaṇakkāṉa tokaikaḷaik koṭuttu avaṉai vilaikku vāṅki viṭukiṉṟārkaḷ! aṅṅaṉam vilaikku vāṅkappaṭṭa maṇamakaṉ mēlumēlun taṉ maṉaiviyiṉ vaḻiyē poruḷ paṟikkum pēravāvuṭaiyaṉāyp pōtaliṉ, avaṉtaṉ maṉaiyāḷai iṭaiyaṟātu tuṉpuṟuttukiṉṟāṉ. ataṉāl avviruvark kuḷḷum uyarnta kātalaṉpu illātākiṉṟatu. koḻunaṉum maṉaiviyum aṉpilarāṉāl ilvāḻkkai veṟum pāḻākiṉṟatu. iṉip poruḷ koṭuttu maṇamakaṉaip peṟa vaḻi ilātavarō tamatu cātiyaik kaṭantu tāṇṭa māṭṭārāy icaiyātavaṉukkuṅ kiḻavaṉukkum peṇṇaik koṭuttut  tuṉpuṟukiṉṟaṉar! taṉ kaḻuttiṟkuk kayiṟu tāṉēyiṭṭukkoṇṭatupōla uṇmaiyil illāta cātivēṟṟumaiyiṉait tāmākavē vakuttukkoṇṭu ivarkaḷ iṅṅaṉamellān tuṉpuṟu vatēṉ! nallarāyt tūyarāy iruppār ekkuṭip piṟantavarāyiruppiṉum avarkku makaḷaik koṭuttalum avarpāl makaṭkōṭalum ellārun tuṇintu ceykuva rāyiṉ, maṉaivikkēṟṟa kaṇavaṉum, kaṇavarkēṟṟa maṉaiviyum vāyppar. ataṉāl, illaṟa vāḻkkai uyarnta tūya iṉpavāḻkkaiyām. ippōtuḷḷa tuṉpaṅkaḷum, maṇamakkaḷai vilaikoṭuttu vāṅkutalum oḻiyum. makaḷiratu kaṟpoḻukkamum evaruñ colla vēṇṭāmalē iṉitu nilaipeṟum.
iṉip, piṟacātiyāriliruntu tūyarāy varuvār kaṭaippiṭiyāyc ceyaṟpālatum oṉṟuṇṭu. iḻintakuṭi oṉṟiṟ piṟantōr ataṟkuriya iḻinta taṉmaikaḷai viṭṭut tūyarāy varumpōtu, tammai mīṇṭum avviḻinta kuṭikku uriya peyarāṟkūṟit tammaittāmē tāḻvu paṭuttikkoḷḷal ākātu. oru tūyavar tammaittāmē uyarttikkoḷḷa vaḻitēṭavēṇṭumē yallāmaṟ, piṟar tammai uyarttuvār eṉṟu nampiyiruttalākātu. ēṉeṉil, nammaṉōr cakkaiyaip piṭittuc cāṟṟai oḻukaviṭum paṉṉāṭaiyaip pōlvar; uyarntār oruvar pāl uḷḷa kuṟṟaṅkaḷai ārāyntu avaṟṟaiyē pēcum nīrar allāmal, avarpāl uḷḷa uyarnta nalaṅkaip pēcum iyalpiṉar allar. ātalāl, ittakaiya tīya makkaḷiṭaiyē tūyarāy uyarvār tamatu iḻikuṭip piṟappaiyuraiyātu, tammaic caivar eṉavum pārppaṉar eṉavuṅ kūṟi yoḻukutalvēṇṭum; tammōṭu uṭaṉiruntu uṇavukoḷḷātār vīṭṭil tāmum uṇaveṭuttal ākātu, immuṟaiyai viṭāppiṭiyāyk koṇṭu oḻukiṉāṟṟāṉ kīḻōril tūyarāy varuvōr uyarakkūṭum ivvāṟu nāḷaṭaiviṟ ceytē pārppaṉaruñ caivarum uyarntaṉar. im muṟaiyaik kaippaṟṟit tūyarāvār ellārun tāmum orumaiyuṟṟup, piṟar ellāraiyun tūyarākki orumaippaṭuttic, cātivēṟṟumaikaḷai aṟavē yoḻittu ellāmvalla muḻumutaṟ kaṭavuḷākiya tantaikku iṉiya putalvarāy iṉitu vāḻvārāka!

cāti vēṟṟumaiyum pōliccaivarum
- muṟṟum -
mutaṟ patippu mukavurai
ki.pi. 1921 ām āṇṭu mārkaḻit tiṅkaḷil yām yāḻppāṇattil uḷḷa aṉparkaḷāl aḻaikkappaṭṭu, aṅkē virivurai nikaḻttac ceṉṟu, yāḻppāṇa nakarattum ataṉaiyaṭuttuḷḷa pala ūrkaḷiluñ caivacittāntap poruḷkaḷaiyun tamiḻcciṟappukaḷaiyum eṭuttup pala virivuraikaḷ nikaḻttiṉēm. av virivuraikaḷai yellām peruntiraḷāṉa aṉparkaḷ āṅkāṅku vantukēṭṭu iṉpuṟṟaṉar. kaṭai muṟaiyāka yām yāḻppāṇattai viṭṭup puṟappaṭutaṟkuc cila nāṭkaḷiṉmuṉ, aḵtāvatu ki.pi. 1922 ām āṇṭu taittiṅkaḷ mutalnāḷ aṉṟu yāḻppāṇa nakaramaṇṭapattil mālai 6 maṇikkut tuvaṅkit tamiḻar nākarikam eṉṉum poruḷai virittup pēciṉēm. tamiḻar nākarikattiṉ varalāṟukaḷai avvirivurai putumuṟai ārāycciyāl naṉkeṭuttu viḷakkiṉamaiyāl, avaṟṟai āṇṭuk kuḻumiyiruntu kēṭṭa aṟivuṭai avaiyattār ellārum av virivuraiyai mika viyantu pārāṭṭiṉar. av virivuraiyiṉ arumai ilaṅkaiyilum, yāḻppāṇattu naṉmakkaḷ mikutiyāyc ceṉṟu kuṭiyēṟi vāḻum malāy nāṭukaḷilum viraintu paravalāyiṟṟu. yāḻppāṇam, kantarōṭaiyiliruntu ceṉṟu malāy nāṭṭil ireṅkāṉ eṉṉum ūriṟ pukaivaṇṭinilai utavit talaivarāy (Assistant station Master) aluvalil amarntirunta tiruvāḷar na.ci. kantaiyā avarkaḷ caivacittānta uṇmaikaḷaiyun tamiḻiṉ arumperuñ ciṟappukaḷaiyum nāṭeṅkum paravac ceyvatil nirampak karuttūṉṟiṉavarkaḷātaliṉ tamiḻar nākarikam eṉṉum av virivuraiyai oru nūlāka acciṭṭu veḷiyiṭumpaṭi emmaik kēṭṭuk koṇṭatōṭu, cekamat eṉṉum ūril araciṉar maruttuva viṭutiyil (Government Hospital) aluval pārkkun tiruvāḷar kumāracāmi avarkaḷai ūkki, avarkaḷum avarkaḷiṉ naṇpar cilarumāka oruṅku cērntu annūṟ patippuc celavukku irunūṟu rūpā poruḷutavi ceyyumāṟum purintiṭṭārkaḷ. tamiḻil ittakaiya putumuṟai ārāycci nūlkaḷ veḷivarutaṟku utavi ceytaliṉuñ ciṟanta aṟam vēṟu illai. ivvuyarnta aṟattaic ceytuvarun tirukkantaiyā avarkaṭkum avarkaḷiṉ naṇpar tiruk kumāracāmi avarkaṭkum avarkaḷōṭu ottu utavi ceyta maṟṟa naṇparkaṭkum yām peritum naṉṟi celuttukiṉṟēm.
kalvivaḷarcciyiṉ poruṭṭup pala naṉmuyaṟcikaḷai yām iṭaiviṭātu ceytuvarutalāl, tamiḻar nākarikam eṉṉum itaṉai eḻuti veḷiyiṭutaṟku amayam vāyātu ēṟakkuṟaiya oṉṟarai āṇṭukaḷ kaḻintaṉa. piṟaku, nāṭṭukkōṭṭaic ceṭṭimār muṉṉēṟṟattiṉ poruṭṭu veḷiyiṭappaṭun taṉavaiciya ūḻiyaṉ eṉṉum itaḻil (ki.pi. 1923 ām āṇṭu āṭittiṅkaḷ 12 ām nāḷ) ataṉ talaivar vēḷāḷar uṇmai varalāṟukaḷai aṟiyāmal avaraic cūttirar eṉṟu ikaḻnatu, tam marapiṉaril uḷḷa āṭavarkaṭku vēḷāḷa marapiṉariliruntu peṇkaḷ eṭuttu maṇañ ceyvittuk koḷḷutalaippaṟṟip pēciyaviṭattut, tām vaiciya vakuppiṉarākalāṉ tammavar tammiṟṟāḻnta cūttira vakuppiṉarāṉa vēḷāḷariṭamiruntu peṇkaḷ eṭuttu maṇañ ceytu koḷḷal muṟaiyēyām eṉṟu eḻutiya kaṭṭurai oṉṟaik kaṇṭēm.
appōtu namakku naṇparāyuḷḷa caiva vēḷāḷar cilar vēḷāḷariṉ paṇṭai uṇmai varalāṟukaḷai ārāyntu oru kaṭṭurai virainteḻutumpaṭi emmaip perituvēṇṭiṉar. paṇṭaikkālan toṭṭu nam centamiḻ makkaḷil nākarikattāṟ ciṟantu vāḻntu, tamiḻmoḻiyaiyuñ civa vaḻipāṭṭaiyum nilainiṟutti āriyaraiyun tirutti nalvaḻip paṭuttiṉōr vēḷāḷarē eṉpatu ematu ārāycciyil naṉku pulap paṭṭamaiyāl, ‘vēḷāḷar yāvar? eṉpataṉai viḷakkukaiyil tamiḻaratu nākarikat taiyum uṭaṉ viḷakka vēṇṭuvatu iṉṟiyamaiyātatāyt tāṉē vantu kūṭiṟṟu. ākavē, yām muṉṉar eḻutak kuṟittirunta tamiḻar nākarikam eṉpataṉaiyum itaṉkaṇ virittu viḷakka iṭam peṟṟēm. āriyar ivvintiya nāṭṭiṟ pukum muṉṉarē tamiḻar nākarika vāḻkkaiyil mutirccipeṟṟu niṉṟu, piṉṉart tammoṭu vantu kalanta avvāriyarait tiruttiṉamai itaṉkaṇ naṉkeṭuttuk kāṭṭappaṭṭirukkiṉṟatu. paḻantamiḻ nūlārāycciyiṉṟi, vaṭa nūṟ payiṟci oṉṟēyuṭaiyār āriyarai uyarttut tamiḻarai iḻittuk kūṟuvaṉavellām poruntāmaiyun, tamiḻarē evvāṟṟāṉum uyarn tōrātalum ivaitammai ārāyātārkkup putumaiyāyt tōṉṟiṉum, yām ārāyntu cāṉṟukaḷōṭu uraippaṉa vaṟṟai naṭunilai vaḻātu niṉṟu kāṇpārkku em uraiyiṉ meymmai tāṉē viḷaṅkutal tiṇṇam.
- maṟaimalai aṭikaḷ
iraṇṭām patippu mukavurai
ivviraṇṭām patippiṟ pala putiyakuṟippukaḷ viritteḻutic cērkkappaṭṭirukkiṉṟaṉa. muṟpatippil iruntavaikaḷiṟ ciṟcila tiruttaṅkaḷuñ ceyyappaṭiruk kiṉṟaṉa. ivvāṟaṟṟāl innūl muṉṉaiyiṉum orupaṅku kūṭapperukiviṭṭatu.
iṉit tamiḻaruṭ ciṟanta vēḷāḷar nākarikattai viḷakkavē tamiḻaratu nākarikamun tāṉē peṟappaṭumākalāṉum, mutaṟ patippukku vaitta `tamiḻar nākarikam’ allatu `vēḷāḷar yāvar?  eṉṉum irupeyarkaḷ vaḻaṅkutaṟku iṭar payattalāṉum ip patippukku avviraṇṭaiyum uḷḷaṭakkiya `vēḷāḷar nākarikam eṉpataṉaip peyarāka amaittām.
pallāvaram							        maṟaimalaiyaṭikaḷ
ki.pi. 1927

Preface to the Second Edition
The first edition of this work was published in November, 1923 and 500 copies of it were sold within four years - a period too short for the sale of a Tamil book of this kind which in contradistinction to the current fashion for a mixed kind of Tamil prose, is written in a scrupulously pure Tamil style, setting forth at the same time views of a revolutionary character in the sphere of social, religious and historical ideas of the Tamil people. In point of truth the views themselves are neither strange nor revolutionary, being the outcome of a comparative study carefully made of both Tamil and Sanscritr literature. If they appear strange surely they must appear so, to many it is because their knowledge is partial: and where that knowledge claims to be derived from the literatures of the two languages, it does not go beyond the pale of modern Puranic literature of the two.
No doubt, beneath the legendary accounts of Puranas critics of trenchant intellect - such are rarely to be met with in this country, may detect facts of great value for studying the history of ancient people, but others, who are untrained in the critical and historical methods of looking at things and events, take every bit of them as so many literal truths. People in every country, befopre they get the benefits of an excellent education, are moree credulous than children, since children cannot rest satisfied in a blind belief with respect to a certain thing or an occurrence, unless they receive satisfying answers to all they want to know about the one or the other. This, the self-interested persons know full well and such of them as are versed in the knowledge of a language know also how to work upon the people’s fancy by making that language as a medium for their artifices. This was how the huge body of Puranic literature came to be written first in Sanscrit and then translated into Tamil and other living languages in India. Sanscrit being the dead and not the spoken language understood by people, the authors of the Puranas adopted it is the means best suited to their purpose and said in them all that was to be said in their own interest and to the great disadvantage of the people. And as if to give a finishing and successful stroke to their artifices, not only did they uniformly conceal their human authorship of the Puranas, but they also attributed the composition of which invariably to Divine Beings such as Siva and Narayana. Being Divine utterances their contents were taught to be unquestionable under penalty of hell-fire to unbelievers. Having thus established the unquestionable authority of the Puranas, their authors, selecting for the basis a few cardinal facts, did their work excellently by exaggerating them so much out of all proportion, twisting them so much out of their shape, and combining them so much out of all hormony, that, to the degree they succeeded in the abnormal treatment of the subject, the legends appealed the more strongly to the minds of the credulous and led their belief to take deep root in them. As a consequence of this artifice people have become slaves to blind faith and ineradicable supersitition and they completely distrust that there can be anything as reason to question about their truth or untruth. Nay the very attitude of a person who dares to doubt the legend is repugnant to the people and they are sure to look upon him as their bitterest enemy and heretic. The hold which the Puranas thus have on the mind of the people and the intensity of the prejudice which they foster in their mind against a man who doubts them, one may best learn by nothing the strength of the Puranic belief even in those who have imbibed western education.
Under this deplorable condition it is no wonder that Tamil books written on modern critical and historical lines should seem strange and revolutionary to men of such mental calibre. Still the sale of 500 copies within four years betokens a brightening prospect and authors of my type need not despair of a better condition in future. For man cannot always rest in ignorance. He is ever prompted by an innate desire to learn the nature and constitution of things and ascertain the causes that are at work within the heart of things. He is ever free to think on life and life -struggles and no power can restrain his thought nor can hold it eternally captive. To this freedom of thinking, the spread of western education is contributing its mighty and accelerating influence and we, on our part must do our utmost to bring the people to think for themselves and examine their beliefs in the lightr of reason before communicating them to their youngsters.
The object of this book is to examine some of the important beliefs that have clustered around the mind of the people and to rid it of such beliefs as are likely to mislead them or to hamper their progress in the path of right knowledge.
In the first place attention is directed to Velalas the civilized agricultural class of the Tamils, and to their origin, and organization. From references made to them, their occupation and social rank, in Tholkappiam the most ancient and existing Tamil work in all its entirety, the age of which goes back to 3500 B.C., it is shown that at a time when all the people except those who lived all along the equatorial regions, were leading the life of hunters or nomads, these Velalas attained perfection in the art of agriculture, built towns and strong forts, had been priests, kings and traders and by means of navigation occupied the whole of India settling in rich and fertile countries along the coasts and river - sides.
When the Aryan hordes came from the north-west of Punjab and poured forth into the interior, it was the ten velala kings then ruling in the north that stopped their advance (See Ragozin’s Vedic India.). When after a while, the intellectual section of the Aryan nomads found it impossible to get admission into the Tamilian territories by combating with their kings, they sought it by peaceful means and were thereafter accorded a cordial reception and admitted to a high rank in the Tamilian society. To the few stray hymns brought by the Aryans, the Tamil kings added a greater number of their own: and, in imitation of their four Tamil vedas which treated of practical ethics, political economy, love and heavenly bliss, they classified the hymns into four books and called them ‘Vedas’. That the word ‘Veda’ is a pure Tamil one, is etymologically proved in the body of this work, by grouping a number of pure Tamil words all having a single root ‘Ve’ and cognate in meaning, After a century of laborious research in linguistic matters, oriental scholars in Europe and America are just now coming to recognise that not only was the Taqmilian civilization prior to the Aryan but it was also much superior to it and was the great formative force that modelled it after its own.
(see prof. Rapson’s Ancient India).
In spite of the repeated efforts which the Taqmil kings made, as is manifest from the Chandogya, Kaushi taki and other upansihads, to divert the attention of the Aryan priests from costly rituals supposed to bring only transient benefits, to a contgemplation of eternal verities and problems of philosophy, the priests were persisting in the performance of bloddy sacrifices so much so that as time went on their conduct became more and more revolting to the delicate feelings of the humanitarian Velals.
So long as the Tamil kings and rich trading communities yielded to the wishes and devices of the Aryan priests and lavished their wealth on rituals, the latter pretended to treat them with utmost respect by designating them the Kshatriyas and Vaisyas, while in fact they were positing themselves openly at the zenith in the scale of castes and casting down others secretly much below. But from the moment the kings and nobles and others began to suspect the motives of the Aryan priests, these supercilious parasites gave up their bloody sacrifices but devised other effective means to such the wealth of the Tamilians. At first they brought all the Tamils under the three denomination of Kshatriya, Vaisya and Sudra, formulated rigid rules exclusively for each and ordained numerous ceremonies toeach, doing all these, byu means of a dead language Sanscrit, as if in the interest of the people’s welfare both here and hereafter, but in fact to establish their supremacy among them and make their priestly help seem indispensable to the people for consultin them on such rules and conducting the ceremonies prescribed for each. In this design the Aryan priests succeeded so well, that the Tamils whether kings or nobles, rich or poor, learned or ignorant, all have become thorough slaves no only to the Aryan priests but also to all who have joined the Aryan fold and bear the name of brahmin. After this the further work of vilifying the Tamils was made much easier, and all those who, in course of time, styled themselves brahmins discovered it, to their great benefit and glory, to efface the three grades of distinctions into which their predecessors classed the Tamils and to put them all together under the generic term ‘Sudra’ which means but the contemptuous menials as a whole. But in the Tamil country nobody will call himself a Sudra, or a Vaisya or a Kshatriya. The Tamils are either agriculturists or traders, artisans or labourers; every class of people follows a hereditary profession and calls themselves by the name of that profession. But quite recently a great mania has taken possession of some classes of people whose professions though much useful are looked upon as low by brahmins and their imitators, to bring themselves under the Aryan appellations of brahmin, kshatriya and vaisya and escape being called the Sudra. This mania is setting one class against the other and breeds enmity and arrogance in each. All this mischief and injustice of classifying the Tamils who are above such caste iniquities, are exposed in the body of this treatise and remedial measures suggested.
Unfortunately owing to the influfence of brahmins, European scholars devoted their attention exclusively to Sanscrit and have acquired the most amazing mastery in it. Had they spent half so much labour on ancient Tamil classics, the result would have been more fruitful, more accurate and more enlightening in the study of ancient Indian history, Philosophy and religion.My truth dispel falsehood and love overcome hate and God who is both truth and lvoe illumine our understanding!
The Sacred Order of love,	Vedachalam
Pallavaram 20, July, 1927

1. vēḷāḷar vāḻkkai
ippōtirukkun tamiḻnūlkaḷil mikap paḻaiyatākiya tolkāppiyattil vēḷāḷarkaḷum avarkaḷukkē ciṟappāka uriya uḻavutoḻilum oruṅkēvaittuc collap paṭutal peritum niṉaiviṟ patikkaṟpāṟṟu. tolkāppiyam aiyāyirattu ainnūṟu āṇṭukaḷukku muṟpaṭṭa nūlākaiyāl, atiṟ kuṟippiṭṭa vēḷāḷarkaḷ annūlukku muṟpaṭṭa kālat tilēyē aḵtāvatu āṟāyira āṇṭukaḷukku muṉṉarēyē uḻavu toḻilaik kaṇṭaṟintu perukki, ataṉāl tāmum nākarikattiṟ ciṟantu, piṟaraiyuñ ciṟappuṟac ceytu vāḻntamai pulaṉām. āṭu māṭu mēykkun toḻilai mēṟkoṇṭu, ovvoru kālattil ovvōriṭattiṟ kuṭiyēṟik, kaṭaiciyāka ip parata nāṭṭiṟ pukunta āriyar aññāṉṟu vaṭakkē vāḻnta vēḷāḷariṉ uḻavu toḻiṟ ciṟappum, ataṉāl avar peṟṟa nākarika vāḻkkaiyuṅkaṇṭu viyantu av vēḷāḷarai aṇṭip piḻaikkalāyiṉar. vēḷāḷarun tamakkuḷḷa celvap perukkālum, irakka neñcattālun tampāl vantu tamatu utaviyai avāviya āriyarkku uṇṇa uṇavum uṭukka uṭaiyum irukka iṭamun tantu palavāṟṟāmalum avarkaḷaip pātukāttuvantaṉar. ākavē, vēḷāḷar eṉṉuñ col īkaiyuṭaiyār eṉṉum poruḷil avarkkē vaḻaṅki varalāyiṟṟu. vēḷāṇmai eṉṉuñcol iṅṅaṉam paṇṭutoṭṭu īkaip poruḷil vaḻaṅkutal, vēḷāṇmai yupakāram īkaiyum viḷampum eṉṉum paḻaiya tivākarac cūttirattāl viḷaṅkum.
uḻavutoḻilō mikavum varuttamāṉa toṉṟu, uḻavu toḻilaic ceypavarkaṭkē varuttam iṉṉateṉpatu teriyum; ataṉaic cevvaiyāyc ceytu muṭippataṟkō muṉpiṉ ārāyum nuṇṇaṟivu vēṇṭum. ātalāl, ataṉaic ceyvārkkē uyarnta aṟivum avvaṟiviṉaip payaṉ
paṭuttum muṟaikaḷum viḷaṅkum. ātaliṉāṟṟāṉ, vēḷāḷarkku irakkamum aṟivum īkaiyun toṉṟutoṭṭu varum iyalpu kaḷākak kūṟappaṭukiṉṟaṉa. tammaiyotta makkaḷ vaṟumai yālum nōyālun tuṉpuṟak kaṇṭāl, avarkkuḷḷa attuṉpattiṉ koṭumaiyiṉai niṉainturuki, avai tammaip poruḷālum maruntālum nīkkavallavarkaḷ vēḷāḷarkaḷē yāvar. piṟar tarum poruḷaip peṟṟut tam meyvaruntāmal illattiruntu iṉitu kālaṅkaḻippavarkkup, piṟar paṭuntuyar teriyātākai yāl aṉṉavar vaṟiyōrukkum nōyāḷarkkum piṟarkkuñ ciṟitum iraṅkār. ātaliṉāṟṟāṉ, vēḷāḷa rallāta piṟar piṟarkku utavi ceytal aritāyirukkiṉṟatu. īkaiyum viruntōmpalum vēḷāḷarkkē ciṟantaṉavāka vaittu nūlkaḷ ōtutalum piṉṉē kāṭṭappaṭum.
iṉit, tammōṭotta makkaḷiṉ tuyar kaḷaitalēyaṉṟi, makkaḷiṉun tāḻnta āṭu māṭu kutirai kōḻi kokku mutalāṉa ciṟṟuyirkaḷ paṭun tuṉpattaiyum naṉkuṇarntu, avaikaṭkut tammālum piṟar tammālun tīṅku nērātapaṭi kollā aṟattai vaḷarkkavalla āṟṟalum vēḷāḷarkkē urittāyiṟṟu.
uyirōṭirukkum makkaḷukkum makkaḷiṉun tāḻnta ciṟṟuyirkaḷukkum utaviyāṟṟutalōṭu amaiyātu, tammaip peṟṟu vaḷarttup periyarākkiya tam mūtātaikaḷiṉ pērutaviyaiyum niṉaintu, avarkaḷ ivvulaka vāḻvait tuṟantu maṟumaiyulakaṅkaṭkuc ceṉṟa piṉṉum, avarkaḷatu uyir tūya piṟaviyaiyō piṟaviyoḻintu iṟaivaṉ tiruvaṭiyaiyō aṭaiyumāṟu iṟaivaṉai vēṇṭi vaḻuttutalum vēḷāḷarkkē urittāyiṟṟu.
iṉit, tamataṟivu vaḷarccikkum muyaṟciyiṉ īṭēṟṟattiṟkum iṉṟiyamaiyāta vaḻivakaikaḷaik kāṭṭi avviraṇṭiṉuñ ciṟanta tammuṉṉōrkaḷ eḻutivaitta nūlkaḷai ōti, avvāṟṟāṟ kalviyaip paravacceytalum vēḷāḷarkkē ciṟantatōr aṟamāyiṟṟu.
ākavē, īkaikkup peyarāṉa vēḷāṇmai eṉṉuñ
col, av vīkaikkuk karuviyāṉa payirttoḻilukkum peyarāyiṟṟu. piṉṉar avviraṇṭu toḻilkaḷaiyum āḷvārāṉa vēḷāḷarkkum atu peyarāyiṟṟu. iv vēḷāḷar ēṉai makkaḷai nōkkic ceyyum īkaiyum viruntōmpalum makkaḷvēḷvi (mānuṭayākam) eṉavum, ēṉaic ciṟṟuyirkaḷaip pātukāttaṟ poruṭṭu  avar mēṟkoṇṭu oḻukuṅkollā aṟam uyirvēḷvi (pūtayākam) eṉavum, iṟantu paṭṭa tam muṉṉōrai nōkkic ceyyum naṉṟikkaṭaṉ teṉ pulattār vēḷvi (pitiryākam) eṉavum, ivvāṟu piṟarkkāṟṟum utavi payaṉtaraṟ poruṭṭu iṟaivaṉai nōkkic ceyyum vaḻipāṭu kaṭavuḷ vēḷvi (tēvayākam) eṉavun tamakkum piṟarkkum aṟivai viḷakki muyaṟciyaip payaṉ peṟuvikkum nūlōtumuṟai kalaivēḷvi (piramayākam) eṉavum āṉṟōrāl vakutturaikkappaṭṭaṉa. eṉavē īkaiyum īkaikkuk karuviyāvaṉavum, īkaiyaip pārāṭṭum naṉṟikkaṭaṉum īkaiyaip payaṉpaṭuttumāṟu vēṇṭum vaḻipāṭum vēḷvi eṉṉum peyarkku iyaipuṭaiyavāy niṟṟal kaṇṭukoḷka. kollā aṟam, ciṟṟuyirkaḷ tattam uṭampukaḷil niṉṟu aṟivu viḷaṅkutaṟku utavi ceyvatākaliṉ, atuvum vēḷvi eṉṉum peyarkkup peritum urimai pūṇṭu niṟṟal kaṇṭu koḷka. iṅṅaṉamāka aivakai vēḷvikaḷaiyum āḷpavarākaliṉ, paṇṭait tamiḻaril uḻavutoḻilāṟ ciṟantu nākarika vāḻkkaiyai vakutta naṉmakkaḷ vēḷāḷar eṉappaṭuvārāyiṉa reṉpatu. 
uḻavutoḻilai yaṟintu ataṉait tiṟamaiyākac ceyyat teriyāta kālaṅkaḷil, makkaḷ uṇṇap pōtumāṉa uṇavum uṭukkac cevvaiyāka uṭaiyum iṉṟi mikavum miṭippaṭṭu uyirvāḻntaṉar; kāṭukaḷilum malaikaḷilum uḷḷa māṉ, marai, kaṭampai, muḷḷampaṉṟi, āṭu, māṭu mutalāṉa vilaṅkiṉaṅkaḷai aḷaviṟanta varuttattōṭu vēṭṭamāṭik koṉṟu, avaṟṟiṉ iṟaicciyaiyum aritiṟ kiṭaitta kāy kaṉi kiḻaṅkukaḷaiyum ayiṉṟu, taḻaikaḷaiyum tōlaiyum uṭuttu, nākarikam iṉṉateṉṟē teriyāmal malaikkukai kaḷilum marap pontukaḷilum iruntu kālaṅkaḻittaṉar. evvaḷavō tēṭittirintum av vilaṅkukaḷum kāy mutaliyaṉavum akappaṭāta kālaṅkaḷiṟ paṭṭiṉiyum paciyumāyiruntu avarkaḷ poṟuttaṟkariya tuṉpattiṟ kaḻitta nāṭkaḷum pala! vēṭṭuva vāḻkkaiyil ippōtum nākarikamiṉṟi uyirvāḻum malai vāṇaruṅ kāṉavarum nirampavum miṭippaṭṭa nilaiyiliruttalai nām iññāṉṟum  nērē ceṉṟu kāṇalām, iṅṅaṉamākap paṇṭaikkālattil vaṟumaiyiṟ kiṭantuḻaṉṟa makkaḷiṉ koṭun tuṉpamellām, uḻavu toḻilai mutaṉmutaṟ kaṇṭaṟinta aṟivuṭai naṉmakkaḷālētām nīṅkiṉa. tamiḻmakkaḷuḷ vēḷāḷar uḻavu toḻilaik kaṇṭaṟinta paṇṭai nāṭkaḷil, ekupati, cālaṭi mutalāṉa ciṟcila nāṭukaḷil uṟainta makkaṭ pakutiyiṉar ciṟcilarait tavira, ēṉaip perumpālār ellārum uḻavu toḻilai aṟiyārāy vēṭṭuva vāḻkkaiyiṟ peritum vaṟumaippaṭṭu vāḻnāṭ kaḻittaṉar. vēḷāḷar uḻavu toḻilai naṭātti nākarikattaip perukkac ceyta paṇṭai nāḷil, āriyar vēṭṭuva vāḻkkaiyilum āṭu māṭu mēykkum iṭaiyar vāḻkkaiyilumē iruntaṉar. ataṉālētāṉ, āriyarum avar vaḻippaṭṭāruñ ceyta nūlkaḷil uḻavu toḻil iḻitturaikkappaṭṭiruppatōṭu, ataṉait tam miṉattavar evaruñ ceytalākāteṉṉuṅ kaṭṭuppāṭuṅ kāṇap paṭukiṉṟatu.1
āṉāṟ, ṟamiḻa vēḷāḷārō avvuḻavu toḻilait tamatu nuṇṇaṟivāṟ kaṇṭuṇarntu ataṉaic cevvaiyākac ceytu vantamaiyāl, attoḻil vēḷāḷarkkē ciṟantatāka vaittu vaṭamoḻi teṉmoḻiyiluḷḷa nūlkaḷellām orumukamāy niṉṟu otturaippavāyiṉa. ivvāṟu uḻavu toḻilai vēḷāḷarkaḷ tōṟṟuvittu piṟaku tāṉ uṇavukkum uṭaikkum uṟaiviṭattiṟkum miṭippaṭṭa tuṉpa vāḻkkai nīṅkiṟṟu. uṇavukkāka vilaṅkiṉaṅkaḷaik kolluṅ kolait toḻil nīṅkiṟṟu; aḷavukkumēl viḷainta nel tuvarai mutalāṉa paṇṭaṅkaḷait tampōṟ paciyāl varuntum ēṉaimakkaṭkum pakuttuk koṭukkum īra neñcamum īkaiyuṅ kiḷaippavāyiṉa; viḷainta paṇṭaṅ kaḷaip pātukāttu vaḻaṅkutaṟku aracaṉum nāṭu nakaraṅkaḷuñ celvamuṅ kalviyum iṉpavāḻkkaiyum iṟaivaṉ vaḻipāṭum oṉṟaṉmēloṉṟāyp perukālāyiṉa. iṉṉum mikuttuk kūṟa vēṇṭuvateṉ! iññāṉṟai makkaṭku vantaḷḷa nākarika vāḻvellām paṇṭum iṉṟum vēḷāḷar kaṇṭaṟintu naṭāttum uḻaviṉāl vantaṉavē yāmeṉṟuṇarntu koḷḷal vēṇṭum. 
iṅṅaṉam ellāt toḻiluñ ciṟantatāy ellār uyirvāḻkkaikkum iṉṟiyamaiyātatāy uḷḷa uḻavu toḻilaip paṇṭutoṭṭu naṭāttivarum vēḷāḷar kolaiyum pulaiyum nīkki nākarikattiṟ ciṟantārāy viḷaṅkutaliṉ avaratu perumai paḻaiya nallāciriyar iyaṟṟiya tamiḻ nūlkaḷiṟ pārāṭṭappaṭṭiruppatōṭu, aḵtu iṉṟukāṟum maṅkātu ēṉai yellā vakuppiṉarkkuriya perumaiyiṉum mikku viḷaṅkāniṟkiṉṟatu. ivarkaḷ tamakkuḷḷa aṟiviṉ ṟiṟattāṟ, kālamaṟintu nilattait tirutti vaḷampaṭutti nel mutalāṉa naṉceyppayirum tuvarai mutalāṉa puṉceyp payirum viḷaivittu, avaṟṟāl varum payaṉkaḷait tāmum uṇṭu piṟarum uṇṇak koṭuttu, yāṭu māṭu mīṉ mutalāṉa maṟṟai uyirkaḷaik kollāmaluṅ, koṉṟu avaṟṟiṉ ūṉait tiṉṉāmalum aruḷoḻukkattil iṉṟu kāṟun talai niṉṟu varukiṉṟārkaḷ. ivarkaḷatu uṇmaip perumaiyiṉai yuḷḷavāṟuṇarntē teyvap pulamait tiruvaḷḷuva nāyaṉār,

  toḻutuṇṭu piṉcel pavar.
